TOOLKIT & METHODOLOGY\n\n## 3.1 TOOL SELECTION PRINCIPLES\n\n- IMAGE TOOL PRIORITY:\n  * For any request involving image creation, modification, style change, or visual design, ALWAYS prefer the Image Tool.\n  * Do NOT describe images purely in text when an image output is expected.\n  * If an image result is required, the Image Tool is mandatory.\n\n- CLI TOOLS PREFERENCE:\n  * Always prefer CLI tools over Python scripts when possible\n  * CLI tools are generally faster and more efficient for:\n    1. Synchronous Commands (blocking):\n      * You can omit `blocking`, as it defaults to true\n      * Use for quick operations that complete within 60 seconds\n      * Commands run directly and wait for completion\n      * Example:\n        <function_calls>\n        <execute-command session_name="default" blocking="true">\n        ls -l\n        </execute-command>\n        </function_calls>\n        (or simply omit the blocking parameter as it defaults to true)\n      * IMPORTANT: Do not use for long-running operations as they will timeout after 60 seconds\n\n  2. Asynchronous Commands (non-blocking):\n      * Use `blocking="false"` for any command that might take longer than 60 seconds or for starting background services\n      * Commands run in background and return immediately\n      * Example:\n        <function_calls>\n        <execute-command session_name="dev" blocking="false">\n        npm run dev\n        </execute-command>\n        </function_calls>\n      * Common use cases:\n        - Development servers (Next.js, React, etc.)\n        - Build processes\n        - Long-running data processing\n        - Background services\n\n- Session Management:\n  * Each command must specify a session_name\n  * Use consistent session names for related commands\n  * Different sessions are isolated from each other\n  * Example: Use "build" session for build commands, "dev" for development servers\n  * Sessions maintain state between commands\n\n- Command Execution Guidelines:\n  * For commands that might take longer than 60 seconds, ALWAYS use run_async="true"\n  * Do not rely on increasing timeout for long-running commands\n  * Use proper session names for organization\n  * Chain commands with && for sequential execution\n  * Use | for piping output between commands\n  * Redirect output to files for long-running processes\n\n- Avoid commands requiring confirmation; actively use -y or -f flags for automatic confirmation\n- Avoid commands with excessive output; save to files when necessary\n- Chain multiple commands with operators to minimize interruptions and improve efficiency:\n  1. Use && for sequential execution: `command1 && command2 && command3`\n  2. Use || for fallback execution: `command1 || command2`\n  3. Use ; for unconditional execution: `command1; command2`\n  4. Use | for piping output: `command1 | command2`\n  5. Use > and >> for output redirection: `command > file` or `command >> file`\n- Use pipe operator to pass command outputs, simplifying operations\n- Use non-interactive `bc` for simple calculations, Python for complex math; never calculate mentally\n- Use `uptime` command when users explicitly request sandbox status check or wake-up\n\n## 3.3 CODE DEVELOPMENT PRACTICES\n- CODING:\n  * Must save code to files before execution; direct code input to interpreter commands is forbidden\n  * Write Python code for complex mathematical calculations and analysis\n  * Use search tools to find solutions when encountering unfamiliar problems\n  * For index.html, use deployment tools directly, or package everything into a zip file and provide it as a message attachment\n  * When creating web interfaces, always create CSS files first before HTML to ensure proper styling and design consistency\n  * For images, use real image URLs from sources like unsplash.com, pexels.com, pixabay.com, giphy.com, or wikimedia.org instead of creating placeholder images; use placeholder.com only as a last resort\n\n- WEBSITE DEPLOYMENT:\n  * Only use the \'deploy\' tool when users explicitly request permanent deployment to a production environment\n  * The deploy tool publishes static HTML+CSS+JS sites to a public URL using S3 web Pages\n  * If the same name is used for deployment, it will redeploy to the same project as before\n  * For temporary or development purposes, serve files locally instead of using the deployment tool\n  * When editing HTML files, always share the preview URL provided by the automatically running HTTP server with the user\n  * The preview URL is automatically generated and available in the tool results when creating or editing HTML files\n  * When deploying, ensure all assets (images, scripts, stylesheets) use relative paths to work correctly\n\n- PYTHON EXECUTION: Create reusable modules with proper error handling and logging. Use -nopgbrk to remove page breaks\n  2. pdfinfo: Get PDF metadata\n    - Use to check PDF properties\n    - Extract page count and dimensions\n  3. pdfimages: Extract images from PDFs\n    - Use -j to convert to JPEG\n    - Use for text replacement\n    - Use for pattern matching\n- File Analysis:\n  1. file: Determine file type\n  2. wc: Count words/lines\n  3. head/tail: View file parts\n  4. less: View large files\n- Data Processing:\n  1. jq: JSON processing\n    - Use -n to specify number of lines\n    - Use -f to follow file changes\n  3. awk: Pattern scanning and processing\n    - Use for column-based data processing\n    - Use for complex text transformations\n  4. find: Locate files and directories\n    - Use for precise text matching\n  2. Combine with CLI tools for powerful searches\n  3. Save complex patterns to files for reuse\n  4. Test patterns with small samples first\n  5. Each section contains specific, actionable subtasks based on complexity - use only as many as needed, no more\n3. Each task should be specific, actionable, and have clear completion criteria\n4. MUST actively work through these tasks one by one, checking them off as completed\n5. Adapt the plan as needed while maintaining its integrity as your execution compass\n\n## 5.2 TODO.MD FILE STRUCTURE AND USAGE\nThe todo.md file is your primary working document and action plan:\n\n1. Contains the complete list of tasks you MUST complete to fulfill the user\'s request\n2. Format with clear sections, each containing specific tasks marked with [ ] (incomplete) or [x] (complete)\n3. Each task should be specific, actionable, and have clear completion criteria\n4. MUST actively work through these tasks one by one, checking them off as completed\n5. Before every action, consult your todo.md to determine which task to tackle next\n6. The todo.md serves as your instruction set - if a task is in todo.md, you are responsible for completing it\n7. Update the todo.md as you make progress, adding new tasks as needed and marking completed ones\n8. Never delete tasks from todo.md - instead mark them complete with [x] to maintain a record of your work\n9. Once ALL tasks in todo.md are marked complete [x], you MUST call either the \'complete\' state or \'ask\' tool to signal task completion\n10. SCOPE CONSTRAINT: Focus on completing existing tasks before adding new ones; avoid continuously expanding scope\n11. CAPABILITY AWARENESS: Only add tasks that are achievable with your available tools and capabilities\n12. FINALITY: After marking a section complete, do not reopen it or add new tasks unless explicitly directed by the user\n13. STOPPING CONDITION: If you\'ve made 3 consecutive updates to todo.md without completing any tasks, reassess your approach and either simplify your plan or **use the \'ask\' tool to seek user guidance.**\n14. COMPLETION VERIFICATION: Only mark a task as [x] complete when you have concrete evidence of completion\n15. SIMPLICITY: Keep your todo.md lean and direct with clear actions, avoiding unnecessary verbosity or granularity\n\n## 5.3 EXECUTION PHILOSOPHY\nYour approach is deliberately methodical and persistent:\n\n1. Operate in a continuous loop until explicitly stopped\n2. Execute one step at a time, following a consistent loop: evaluate state → select tool → execute → provide narrative update → track progress\n3. Every action is guided by your todo.md, consulting it before selecting any tool\n4. Thoroughly verify each completed step before moving forward\n5. **Provide Markdown-formatted narrative updates directly in your responses** to keep the user informed of your progress, explain your thinking, and clarify the next steps. Use headers, brief descriptions, and context to make your process transparent.\n6. CRITICALLY IMPORTANT: Continue running in a loop until either:\n  - Using the **\'ask\' tool (THE ONLY TOOL THE USER CAN RESPOND TO)** to wait for essential user input (this pauses the loop)\n  - Using the \'complete\' tool when ALL tasks are finished\n7. For casual conversation:\n  - Use **\'ask\'** to properly end the conversation and wait for user input (**USER CAN RESPOND**)\n8. For tasks:\n  - Use **\'ask\'** when you need essential user input to proceed (**USER CAN RESPOND**)\n  - Provide **narrative updates** frequently in your responses to keep the user informed without requiring their input\n  - Use \'complete\' only when ALL tasks are finished\n9. MANDATORY COMPLETION:\n  - IMMEDIATELY use \'complete\' or \'ask\' after ALL tasks in todo.md are marked [x]\n  - NO additional commands or verifications after all tasks are complete\n  - NO further exploration or information gathering after completion\n  - NO redundant checks or validations after completion\n  - FAILURE to use \'complete\' or \'ask\' after task completion is a critical error\n\n## 5.4 TASK MANAGEMENT CYCLE\n1. STATE EVALUATION: Examine Todo.md for priorities, analyze recent Tool Results for environment understanding, and review past actions for context\n2. TOOL SELECTION: Choose exactly one tool that advances the current todo item\n3. EXECUTION: Wait for tool execution and observe results\n4. **NARRATIVE UPDATE:** Provide a **Markdown-formatted** narrative update directly in your response before the next tool call. Include explanations of what you\'ve done, what you\'re about to do, and why. Use headers, brief paragraphs, and formatting to enhance readability.\n5. PROGRESS TRACKING: Update todo.md with completed items and new tasks\n6. METHODICAL ITERATION: Repeat until section completion\n7. SECTION TRANSITION: Document completion and move to next section\n8. COMPLETION: IMMEDIATELY use \'complete\' or \'ask\' when ALL tasks are finished\n\n# 6. CONTENT CREATION\n\n## 6.1 WRITING GUIDELINES\n- Write content in continuous paragraphs using varied sentence lengths for engaging prose; avoid list formatting\n- Use prose and paragraphs by default; otherwise state “no text”\n- For image editing, clearly state what changes and what must remain unchanged\n- Request the image first if an edit is requested without a provided image\n- Ask clarification questions only when essential information is missing\n\n# 7. COMMUNICATION & USER INTERACTION\n\n## 7.1 CONVERSATIONAL INTERACTIONS\nFor casual conversation and social interactions:\n  - ALWAYS use **\'ask\'** tool to end the conversation and wait for user input (**USER CAN RESPOND**)\n  - NEVER use \'complete\' for casual conversation\n  - Keep responses friendly and natural\n  - Adapt to user\'s communication style\n  - Ask follow-up questions when appropriate (**using \'ask\'**)\n  - Show interest in user\'s responses\n\n## 7.2 COMMUNICATION PROTOCOLS\n- **Core Principle: Communicate proactively, directly, and descriptively throughout your responses.**\n\n- **Narrative-Style Communication:**\n  * Integrate descriptive Markdown-formatted text directly in your responses before, between, and after tool calls\n  * Use a conversational yet efficient tone that conveys what you\'re doing and why\n  * Structure your communication with Markdown headers, brief paragraphs, and formatting for enhanced readability\n  * Balance detail with conciseness - be informative without being verbose\n\n- **Communication Structure:**\n  * Begin tasks with a brief overview of your plan\n  * Provide context headers like `## Planning`, `### Researching`, `## Creating File`, etc.\n  * Before each tool call, explain what you\'re about to do and why\n  * After significant results, summarize what you learned or accomplished\n  * Use transitions between major steps or sections\n  * Maintain a clear narrative flow that makes your process transparent to the user\n\n- **Message Types & Usage:**\n  * **Direct Narrative:** Embed clear, descriptive text directly in your responses explaining your actions, reasoning, and observations\n  * **\'ask\' (USER CAN RESPOND):** Use ONLY for essential needs requiring user input (clarification, confirmation, options, missing info, validation). This blocks execution until user responds.\n  * Minimize blocking operations (\'ask\'); maximize narrative descriptions in your regular responses.\n- **Deliverables:**\n  * Attach all relevant files with the **\'ask\'** tool when asking a question related to them, or when delivering final results before completion.\n  * Always include representable files as attachments when using \'ask\' - this includes HTML files, presentations, writeups, visualizations, reports, and any other viewable content.\n  * For any created files that can be viewed or presented (such as index.html, slides, documents, charts, etc.), always attach them to the \'ask\' tool to ensure the user can immediately see the results.\n  * Share results and deliverables before entering complete state (use \'ask\' with attachments as appropriate).\n  * Ensure users have access to all necessary resources.\n\n- Communication Tools Summary:\n  * **\'ask\':** Essential questions/clarifications. BLOCKS execution. **USER CAN RESPOND.**\n  * **text via markdown format:** Frequent UI/progress updates. NON-BLOCKING. **USER CANNOT RESPOND.**\n  * Include the \'attachments\' parameter with file paths or URLs when sharing resources (works with both \'ask\').\n  * **\'complete\':** Only when ALL tasks are finished and verified. Terminates execution.\n\n- Tool Results: Carefully analyze all tool execution results to inform your next actions. **Use regular text in markdown format to communicate significant results or progress.**\n\n## 7.3 ATTACHMENT PROTOCOL\n- **CRITICAL: ALL VISUALIZATIONS MUST BE ATTACHED:**\n  * When using the \'ask\' or \'complete\' tool, ALWAYS attach ALL visualizations, markdown files, charts, graphs, reports, and any viewable content created.\n  * This includes but is not limited to: HTML files, PDF documents, markdown files, images, data visualizations, presentations, reports, dashboards, UI mockups and externally accessible results.\n  * NEVER mention a visualization or viewable content without attaching it\n  * If you\'ve created multiple visualizations, attach ALL of them\n  * Always make visualizations available to the user BEFORE marking tasks as complete\n  * For web applications or interactive content, always attach the main HTML file\n  * When creating data analysis results, charts must be attached, not just described\n  * Remember: If the user should SEE it, you must ATTACH it with the \'ask\' or \'complete\' tool\n  * Verify that ALL visual outputs have been attached before proceeding\n\n- **Attachment Checklist:**\n  * Data visualizations (charts, graphs, plots)\n  * Web interfaces (HTML/CSS/JS files)\n  * Reports and documents (PDF, HTML)\n  * Presentation materials\n  * Images and diagrams\n  * Interactive dashboards\n  * Analysis results with visual components\n  * UI designs and mockups\n  * Any file intended for user viewing or interaction\n\n\n# 8. COMPLETION PROTOCOLS\n\nCRITICAL: Every final response MUST use the \'ask\' or \'complete\' tool call, regardless of whether todo.md is used, plain text responses are NOT valid. All generated files and URLs MUST be included in the \'attachments\' and \'urls\' attributes respectively.\n\nCRITICAL: Text you write before or between tool calls is NOT shown to the user. When you use the \'ask\' or \'complete\' tool, you MUST include the full deliverable (the complete message, draft, or answer, not a reference to it) inside the tool\'s text argument.\n\n## 8.1 TERMINATION RULES\n- IMMEDIATE COMPLETION:\n  * As soon as ALL tasks in todo.md are marked [x], you MUST use \'complete\' or \'ask\'\n  * No additional commands or verifications are allowed after completion\n  * No further exploration or information gathering is permitted\n  * No redundant checks or validations are needed\n\n- COMPLETION VERIFICATION:\n  * Verify task completion only once\n  * If all tasks are complete, immediately use \'complete\' or \'ask\'\n  * Do not perform additional checks after verification\n  * Do not gather more information after completion\n\n- COMPLETION TIMING:\n  * Use \'complete\' or \'ask\' immediately after the last task is marked [x]\n  * No delay between task completion and tool call\n  * No intermediate steps between completion and tool call\n  * No additional verifications between completion and tool call\n\n- COMPLETION CONSEQUENCES:\n  * Failure to use \'complete\' or \'ask\' after task completion is a critical error\n  * The system will continue running in a loop if completion is not signaled\n  * Additional commands after completion are considered errors\n  * Redundant verifications after completion are prohibited\n\n# 9. Never assume a service is available without checking first.\n\n# 11. BROWSER AUTOMATION TOOLS\n\n- BROWSER CONTROL: All browser interaction goes through `browser-tool <action> [args...]` via `execute-command`.\n  * The browser (Chromium) is already running. Each command connects, acts, observes, and disconnects.\n\n- WORKFLOW (observe-act loop):\n  1. Run an action (e.g. navigate, click, fill). The tool automatically observes the page after every action.\n  2. Read the output: URL, title, interactive elements list, accessibility tree, and screenshot path.\n  3. Reference elements by their `[index]` number from the elements list (e.g. `click "[3]"`).\n  4. Use `see-image` on the screenshot path when you need to verify visual layout or images.\n\n- AVAILABLE COMMANDS:\n  * `observe` - View current page state without acting\n  * `navigate <url>` - Go to a URL\n  * `click <selector>` - Click an element (use `[N]` for element index, or CSS selector)\n  * `fill <selector> <text>` - Clear a field and fill it with text\n  * `type <selector> <text>` - Type text character by character\n  * `press <key>` - Press a key (Enter, Tab, Escape, etc.)\n  * `select <selector> <value>` - Select a dropdown option\n  * `scroll_down [px]` / `scroll_up [px]` - Scroll (default 500px)\n  * `scroll_to_text <text>` - Scroll until text is visible\n  * `go_back` - Navigate back\n  * `wait [seconds]` - Wait (default 2s)\n  * `extract_text [selector]` - Extract text content from page or element\n  * `extract_links [selector]` - Extract all links\n  * `screenshot [filename]` - Take a manual screenshot\n  * `dismiss_overlay` - Dismiss cookie banners or modals\n  * `js <script>` - Execute JavaScript on the page\n\n- CAPTCHA / HUMAN ASSISTANCE: If you encounter a CAPTCHA, verification, or any situation where automated interaction is stuck, MUST use `web-browser-takeover` to hand control to the user.\n\n# 12. If no system-reminder explicitly activates chat mode, you are in agentic mode and MUST use your full toolkit.\n\n## Chat Mode (only when explicitly activated by system-reminder)\n- Use ONLY <ask> tool. Now I'll add documentation, a protocol spec, and a reproducible end-to-end test script.", 'tool_calls': [{'id': 'call_01a1875826187270a04f8b0b', 'type': 'function', 'function': {'name': 'create_file', 'arguments': '{"file_contents":"# rsyncj\\n\\nA secure, rsync-style file synchroniser for Java 25.\\n\\n`rsyncj` copies a local file or directory tree to a remote server and only sends the\\nparts of each file that the server does not already have. Unlike a naive upload it uses\\nthe classic rsync rolling-checksum algorithm, so inserting bytes at the start of a large\\nfile does not force the whole file to be transferred again. Everything runs over TLS with\\nmutual authentication, and the server exposes live JSON statistics that the client can\\nquery through its API.\\n\\n```\\n$ rsyncj sync ./website alice@backup.example.com:8443:/srv/www --delete\\nSynced 1 284 file(s) (1 190 unchanged) in 842 ms\\n  data sent : 4.1 MiB\\n  reused    : 812.6 MiB\\n  efficiency: 99.5%\\n```\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Contents\\n\\n1. [Features](#features)\\n2. [Requirements and build](#requirements-and-build)\\n3. [Quick start](#quick-start)\\n4. [Command line reference](#command-line-reference)\\n5. [How the delta transfer works](#how-the-delta-transfer-works)\\n6. [Security model](#security-model)\\n7. [Statistics API](#statistics-api)\\n8. [Java client API](#java-client-api)\\n9. [Project layout](#project-layout)\\n10. [Testing](#testing)\\n11. [Operational notes and limitations](#operational-notes-and-limitations)\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Features\\n\\n* **Delta synchronisation** – per-block weak (rolling Adler) and strong (truncated\\n  SHA-256) checksums; only literal bytes that the server cannot reconstruct are sent.\\n* **Files or directory trees** – recursive directory sync, empty directories, POSIX\\n  permissions and modification times, optional deletion of extraneous remote files.\\n* **Dry-run mode** – compute and report the delta without writing anything.\\n* **TLS 1.3 / 1.2** with a self-signed server certificate and SSH-style\\n  trust-on-first-use fingerprint pinning (or explicit `--fingerprint` pinning).\\n* **SCRAM-SHA-256 mutual authentication** – the server stores only PBKDF2-derived\\n  verifier keys, never passwords, and the client also verifies the server.\\n* **Path confinement** – every remote path is resolved inside the configured root;\\n  traversal, absolute escapes and NUL bytes are rejected.\\n* **Integrity and atomicity** – every file is rebuilt in a temporary file, verified\\n  against the client\'s SHA-256 and size, then atomically renamed into place.\\n* **JSON statistics** – cumulative counters and per-file records, available over the\\n  authenticated protocol *and* an HTTPS endpoint protected by a bearer token.\\n* **Persistent stats** – counters survive server restarts.\\n* **Java 25** – records, sealed-style switch expressions, virtual threads per\\n  connection, and a small, dependency-light codebase.\\n\\n## Requirements and build\\n\\n* JDK 25 (`java -version` must report 25)\\n* Maven 3.9+\\n\\n```bash\\nmvn clean package\\n```\\n\\nThis produces two runnable fat jars:\\n\\n```\\nrsyncj-server/target/rsyncj-server.jar\\nrsyncj-client/target/rsyncj-client.jar\\n```\\n\\nRun the unit tests with `mvn test`. A full end-to-end test (server, TLS, auth, delta\\ntransfer, deletion, stats) is available as `scripts/e2e-test.sh`.\\n\\n## Quick start\\n\\n### 1. Create a user on the server\\n\\n```bash\\njava -jar rsyncj-server/target/rsyncj-server.jar add-user alice \\\\\\n     --state ./server-state\\n# prompts for a password (or use --password / RSYNCJ_PASSWORD)\\n```\\n\\nThe state directory holds `keystore.p12` (TLS key), `users.json` (SCRAM verifiers),\\n`stats.json` and `stats-token`. All are created with `0600` permissions where the\\nfilesystem supports it.\\n\\n### 2. Start the server\\n\\n```bash\\njava -jar rsyncj-server/target/rsyncj-server.jar serve \\\\\\n     --root /srv/rsyncj-data \\\\\\n     --state ./server-state \\\\\\n     --port 8443 \\\\\\n     --stats-port 8444\\n```\\n\\n```\\nrsyncj/1.0.0 server\\n  root          : /srv/rsyncj-data\\n  listening     : 0.0.0.0:8443 (TLS)\\n  stats api     : https://0.0.0.0:8444/api/stats\\n  stats token   : 3Yk... (also stored in server-state/stats-token)\\n  users         : [alice]\\n  fingerprint   : 38171a2123a40bfb15a4e01f4c71915f7c2546bcb0e349dd33b71b267dbf87ff\\n```\\n\\nOn first connection the client prints the fingerprint and stores it in\\n`~/.rsyncj/known_hosts`. Later connections fail if the server key changes.\\n\\n### 3. Synchronise\\n\\n```bash\\nexport RSYNCJ_PASSWORD=\'correct horse battery staple\'\\n\\n# directory: contents of ./site are mirrored into <root>/www\\njava -jar rsyncj-client/target/rsyncj-client.jar sync ./site alice@server:8443:/www\\n\\n# single file\\njava -jar rsyncj-client/target/rsyncj-client.jar sync ./report.pdf alice@server:8443:/docs/report.pdf\\n\\n# mirror + delete remote files that no longer exist locally\\njava -jar rsyncj-client/target/rsyncj-client.jar sync ./site alice@server:8443:/www --delete\\n\\n# preview only\\njava -jar rsyncj-client/target/rsyncj-client.jar sync ./site alice@server:8443:/www --dry-run --verbose\\n```\\n\\nRemote paths are always relative to the server root, even when written with a leading\\nslash: `/www` means `<root>/www`. A trailing slash on a single-file target means \\"copy\\ninto this directory\\":\\n\\n```bash\\nrsyncj sync ./a.txt alice@server:8443:/inbox/     # -> <root>/inbox/a.txt\\n```\\n\\n## Command line reference\\n\\n### Server\\n\\n```\\nrsyncj-server serve [options]\\nrsyncj-server add-user <name> [--password <pw>] [options]\\nrsyncj-server remove-user <name> [options]\\nrsyncj-server fingerprint [options]\\n\\n  --root <dir>          Writable root (default ./rsyncj-data)\\n  --bind <address>      Listen address (default 0.0.0.0)\\n  --port <n>            TLS sync port (default 8443)\\n  --stats-port <n>      HTTPS stats port (default 8444)\\n  --no-stats-http       Disable the HTTPS stats endpoint\\n  --state <dir>         State directory (default ./rsyncj-state)\\n  --max-file-size <n>   Maximum accepted file size in bytes (default 8 GiB)\\n```\\n\\n### Client\\n\\n```\\nrsyncj sync <source> <user@host[:port]:remotePath> [options]\\nrsyncj stats <user@host[:port]> [options]\\nrsyncj fingerprint <host[:port]>\\n\\nSync options\\n  --delete              Delete remote files that no longer exist locally\\n  --dry-run             Compute the delta but write nothing\\n  --verbose             One line per file\\n  --password <pw>       Password (else RSYNCJ_PASSWORD or an interactive prompt)\\n  --accept-new          Trust an unknown server key on first use (default)\\n  --no-accept-new       Refuse unknown server keys\\n  --fingerprint <hex>   Pin the server certificate SHA-256 fingerprint\\n  --known-hosts <file>  known_hosts location (default ~/.rsyncj/known_hosts)\\n\\nStats options\\n  --json                Print raw JSON\\n  --http                Use the HTTPS stats endpoint instead of the sync protocol\\n  --stats-port <n>      HTTPS stats port (default 8444)\\n  --token <token>       Bearer token for the HTTPS endpoint\\n```\\n\\nExit codes: `0` success, `1` runtime/transfer error, `2` usage error.\\n\\n## How the delta transfer works\\n\\nFor every file the client and server perform a miniature rsync session:\\n\\n1. The client announces the file, its size, mode, mtime and a block size derived from\\n   the file size (`4096` bytes, doubled until the signature table has at most ~32 768\\n   entries, capped at 1 MiB).\\n2. If the server already has the file, it reads it block by block and returns a\\n   **signature table**: for each block, its index, a 32-bit rolling checksum and a\\n   16-byte truncated SHA-256.\\n3. The client walks the new file with a rolling window of one block. At each position\\n   it looks up the rolling checksum in the table. On a hit it verifies the strong\\n   checksum and emits a `COPY <blockIndex>` operation; otherwise it emits one literal\\n   byte and rolls the window forward by one byte. Both steps are O(1), so the whole\\n   file is processed in linear time.\\n4. Literal bytes are streamed in `DATA` frames (64 KiB each); copy operations are tiny\\n   JSON control messages. The server reconstructs the file by reading the referenced\\n   blocks from the old file and appending literal bytes.\\n5. The client computes SHA-256 while streaming and sends it with the final size. The\\n   server verifies both before replacing the destination atomically.\\n\\nBecause the rolling checksum is position-independent, inserting or deleting bytes\\nanywhere in a file only invalidates the blocks around the edit. A 2 GiB file with 100 KiB\\ninserted at the front transfers roughly 100 KiB plus one block.\\n\\nIf the destination is byte-identical, every block is a `COPY`, no `DATA` is sent, and\\nthe server reports the file as *unchanged* without touching it.\\n\\n## Security model\\n\\n| Threat | Mitigation |\\n| --- | --- |\\n| Eavesdropping | TLS 1.3 (1.2 fallback), modern cipher suites from the JDK. |\\n| Server impersonation | Self-signed certificate pinned by SHA-256 fingerprint in `known_hosts`; a changed key is a hard failure. `--fingerprint` allows explicit out-of-band pinning. |\\n| Client impersonation | SCRAM-SHA-256 challenge/response. The password never crosses the wire; the server stores only PBKDF2 verifiers. |\\n| Replay | Fresh 24-byte server and client nonces per connection are bound into the HMAC transcript. |\\n| Password theft from server disk | `users.json` contains `salt`, `iterations`, `storedKey` and `serverKey` only. Neither key can be used to authenticate as the client. |\\n| Path traversal | `PathSafety` strips leading `/`, rejects `..` segments and NUL bytes, normalises, and verifies the result still starts with the root. |\\n| Symlink escape | Symlinks are never followed during traversal or deletion; incoming files are written with `CREATE`/`TRUNCATE` to a fresh temp file. |\\n| Partial/corrupt files | SHA-256 + size verification, `fsync`, then atomic `rename`. A failed transfer leaves the old file untouched. |\\n| Memory exhaustion | Hard 16 MiB frame cap, 1 MiB block cap, configurable maximum file size, bounded signature tables. |\\n| Stats data leakage | HTTPS endpoint requires `Authorization: Bearer <token>` (32 random bytes, `0600` file, constant-time compare). The protocol endpoint requires a full SCRAM session. |\\n| User enumeration | Unknown users receive a random challenge indistinguishable from a wrong password, and the same `auth_failed` error. |\\n\\nThe server also enforces a 180-second socket idle timeout and validates every field of\\nevery control message (sizes, block indexes, block sizes, path lengths).\\n\\n**Key management.** The TLS key pair is generated on first start with the JDK\'s\\nBouncyCastle provider (EC P-256, SHA-256 self-signature, 10-year validity) and stored in\\na PKCS#12 keystore. Back up `keystore.p12` and `users.json` together; losing the\\nkeystore changes the fingerprint and requires clients to re-trust the server.\\n\\n## Statistics API\\n\\nThe server keeps cumulative counters and a per-file record of the last synchronisation.\\nThey are persisted to `stats.json` every 15 seconds and on shutdown, and restored at\\nstartup.\\n\\n### Over the authenticated protocol (client API)\\n\\n```bash\\njava -jar rsyncj-client/target/rsyncj-client.jar stats alice@server:8443\\n```\\n\\n```json\\n{\\n  \\"server\\": {\\n    \\"product\\": \\"rsyncj/1.0.0\\",\\n    \\"protocol\\": 1,\\n    \\"root\\": \\"/srv/rsyncj-data\\",\\n    \\"bind\\": \\"0.0.0.0\\",\\n    \\"port\\": 8443,\\n    \\"startedAt\\": \\"2026-10-29T11:03:28.313916918Z\\",\\n    \\"generatedAt\\": \\"2026-10-29T11:04:38.990826444Z\\",\\n    \\"uptimeSeconds\\": 70\\n  },\\n  \\"sessions\\": { \\"total\\": 6, \\"active\\": 1, \\"authFailures\\": 0 },\\n  \\"syncs\\": {\\n    \\"total\\": 2,\\n    \\"filesSynced\\": 9,\\n    \\"filesSkipped\\": 1,\\n    \\"filesDeleted\\": 0,\\n    \\"bytesReceived\\": 2001200,\\n    \\"bytesReused\\": 1998848,\\n    \\"bytesSent\\": 25747,\\n    \\"deltaEfficiency\\": 0.4997,\\n    \\"errors\\": 0,\\n    \\"lastSyncAt\\": \\"2026-10-29T11:03:41.424129680Z\\"\\n  },\\n  \\"storage\\": { \\"files\\": 5, \\"bytes\\": 2000024 },\\n  \\"files\\": [\\n    {\\n      \\"path\\": \\"backup/big.bin\\",\\n      \\"size\\": 2000000,\\n      \\"mtime\\": \\"2026-10-29T11:02:55Z\\",\\n      \\"lastSyncAt\\": \\"2026-10-29T11:03:41.421Z\\",\\n      \\"sha256\\": \\"9f2c…\\",\\n      \\"bytesReceived\\": 1024,\\n      \\"bytesReused\\": 1998848,\\n      \\"durationMs\\": 118\\n    }\\n  ]\\n}\\n```\\n\\n### Over HTTPS\\n\\n```bash\\nTOKEN=$(cat server-state/stats-token)\\ncurl -sk -H \\"Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN\\" https://server:8444/api/stats | jq\\ncurl -sk -H \\"Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN\\" https://server:8444/api/stats/files | jq\\ncurl -sk https://server:8444/health\\n```\\n\\n`GET /health` is unauthenticated and returns `{\\"status\\":\\"ok\\"}`. All other endpoints\\nreturn `401` without a valid bearer token and `405` for non-GET methods.\\n\\n## Java client API\\n\\nThe client is a library as well as a CLI:\\n\\n```java\\nimport io.rsyncj.client.*;\\nimport java.nio.file.Path;\\n\\nchar[] password = System.getenv(\\"RSYNCJ_PASSWORD\\").toCharArray();\\n\\nClientConfig config = ClientConfig.create(\\"backup.example.com\\", 8443, \\"alice\\", password)\\n        .withHostKeyPolicy(true, Path.of(\\"/home/alice/.rsyncj/known_hosts\\"), null);\\n\\ntry (RsyncClient client = new RsyncClient(config)) {\\n    SyncResult result = client.sync(\\n            SyncRequest.of(Path.of(\\"site\\"), \\"/www\\")\\n                    .withDelete(true)\\n                    .withListener((path, size, sent, reused, skipped) ->\\n                            System.out.printf(\\"%s sent=%d reused=%d%n\\", path, sent, reused)));\\n\\n    System.out.printf(\\"sent %d bytes, reused %d bytes (%.1f%%)%n\\",\\n            result.bytesReceived(), result.bytesReused(), result.deltaEfficiency() * 100);\\n\\n    var stats = client.getStats();          // Jackson JsonNode, same document as above\\n    System.out.println(stats.path(\\"syncs\\").path(\\"deltaEfficiency\\").asDouble());\\n}\\n```\\n\\n`SyncRequest` is immutable; `withDelete`, `withDryRun`, `withVerbose` and `withListener`\\nreturn modified copies. `SyncResult` exposes `files`, `skipped`, `bytesReceived`,\\n`bytesReused`, `deleted`, `durationMs`, `dryRun` and `deltaEfficiency()`.\\n\\n## Project layout\\n\\n```\\nrsyncj/\\n├── pom.xml                     parent POM (Java 25, Jackson, BouncyCastle, JUnit 5)\\n├── rsyncj-common/              protocol, delta algorithm, crypto, path safety\\n│   ├── protocol/               frame codec and control messages\\n│   ├── delta/                  RollingChecksum, BlockSignature, DeltaEncoder\\n│   ├── crypto/                 SCRAM-SHA-256\\n│   └── util/                   PathSafety, Hex\\n├── rsyncj-server/              TLS listener, sessions, receiver, stats, CLI\\n├── rsyncj-client/              TLS client, delta sender, stats client, CLI\\n├── scripts/e2e-test.sh         full end-to-end test\\n└── docs/PROTOCOL.md            wire protocol specification\\n```\\n\\n## Testing\\n\\n```bash\\nmvn test                 # unit tests: rolling checksum, delta round-trip, path safety\\nscripts/e2e-test.sh      # builds, starts a real server, exercises the whole feature set\\n```\\n\\nThe end-to-end script verifies: initial directory sync, byte-for-byte equality,\\ndelta reuse after a large insertion, unchanged re-sync (zero data), `--delete`,\\nsingle-file sync, dry-run, wrong-password rejection, path-traversal rejection,\\nhost-key mismatch detection, HTTPS stats with and without a token, and stats\\npersistence across a restart.\\n\\n## Operational notes and limitations\\n\\n* **Not a drop-in rsync replacement.** The wire protocol is rsync-*inspired*, not\\n  compatible with `rsync(1)` or its daemon.\\n* **Regular files and directories only.** Symlinks, devices, sockets and sparse files\\n  are skipped (and reported in verbose mode). Extended attributes and ACLs are not\\n  transferred.\\n* **Whole-file locking is per path**, not per root; two concurrent syncs writing the\\n  same file are serialised, but a sync and an unrelated local process are not.\\n* **Deletion is opt-in** (`--delete`) and confined to the sync target subtree.\\n* **Stats grow with the number of distinct paths.** For very large trees consider\\n  capping or rotating `stats.json`.\\n* **TLS trust is pinned, not CA-based.** That is deliberate for a self-hosted tool; if\\n  you terminate TLS in a reverse proxy, disable the built-in listener and adapt the\\n  client accordingly.\\n* **Password handling.** Prefer `RSYNCJ_PASSWORD` or the interactive prompt over\\n  `--password`, which is visible in the process list.\\n* **Firewall.** Expose only the sync port (default 8443) and, if needed, the stats port\\n  (8444). The stats port is read-only but still requires the bearer token.\\n\\n## License\\n\\nMIT.\\n","file_path":"rsyncj/README.md"}'}}]}

All control messages are UTF-8\\nJSON objects with a `type` field. File bytes travel in separate binary frames. The\\nconnection is strictly sequential: one request/response exchange at a time, with file\\ndata streamed between `file_begin` and `file_end`.\\n\\n## Framing\\n\\n```\\n+----------------+------------+------------------+\\n| uint32 length  | uint8 type | payload[length]  |\\n+----------------+------------+------------------+\\n```\\n\\n* `length` is the payload length in bytes (big-endian, 0 .. 16 MiB).\\n* `type` is `1` for JSON and `2` for raw file data.\\n* A frame larger than 16 MiB, or an unknown type, terminates the session.\\n\\n## Handshake\\n\\n```\\nclient                                server\\n  |  hello {version, user, clientId}  ->\\n  |                                   <- challenge {salt, iterations, nonce}\\n  |  auth {clientNonce, proof}        ->\\n  |                                   <- auth_ok {sessionId, serverSignature, ...}\\n```\\n\\n* `salt` is 16 random bytes, base64. `iterations` is the PBKDF2 iteration count\\n  (currently 210 000). `nonce` is 24 random bytes, base64.\\n* `saltedPassword = PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256(password, salt, iterations, 256 bits)`\\n* `clientKey      = HMAC-SHA256(saltedPassword, \\"Client Key\\")`\\n* `storedKey      = SHA256(clientKey)`\\n* `serverKey      = HMAC-SHA256(saltedPassword, \\"Server Key\\")`\\n* `authMessage    = \\"rsyncj-auth-v1,\\" + user + \\",\\" + clientNonce + \\",\\" + serverNonce`\\n* `proof          = HMAC-SHA256(storedKey, authMessage)`\\n* `serverSignature= HMAC-SHA256(serverKey, authMessage)`\\n\\nThe server stores `salt`, `iterations`, `storedKey` and `serverKey`. The client verifies\\n`serverSignature`; a mismatch aborts the connection. Unknown users receive a random\\nchallenge and the same `auth_failed` error as a wrong password.\\n\\n## Synchronisation\\n\\n```\\nsync_begin {target, kind: \\"dir\\"|\\"file\\", delete, dryRun}\\n  <- sync_ready {target, kind, dryRun}\\n\\n  for each directory:\\n    dir_begin {path, mtime, mode}\\n      <- dir_done {path}\\n\\n  for each file:\\n    file_begin {path, size, mtime, mode, blockSize}\\n      <- signatures {exists, blockSize, size, blocks: [[index, weak, strongB64], ...]}\\n    (DATA frames and copy messages, in any order)\\n    file_end {sha256, size}\\n      <- file_done {path, size, bytesReceived, bytesReused, skipped, sha256}\\n\\nsync_end {delete, dryRun}\\n  <- sync_result {files, skipped, bytesReceived, bytesReused, deleted, dryRun, durationMs}\\n```\\n\\nOther commands: `stats` → `stats_result {stats}`, `ping` → `pong`, `quit` → `bye`.\\n\\nAny failure produces `error {code, message}` and the server closes the connection.\\nCodes include `auth_failed`, `version_mismatch`, `protocol_error`.\\n\\n### Delta stream\\n\\nBetween `file_begin` and `file_end` the client sends, in file order:\\n\\n* `DATA` frames containing literal bytes (up to 64 KiB each), and\\n* `copy {block}` JSON messages meaning \\"append block `block` of the existing file\\".\\n\\nThe server reads block `i` at offset `i * blockSize` with length\\n`min(blockSize, oldSize - i * blockSize)`. Block indexes outside the old file are\\nrejected. The concatenation of all literal bytes and copied blocks must equal the new\\nfile exactly; this is verified by size and SHA-256 before the file is moved into place.\\n\\n### Signature table\\n\\nFor each block of the existing file:\\n\\n* `weak` – 32-bit rolling checksum: `a = Σ x_i mod 65536`,\\n  `b = Σ (n-i)·x_i mod 65536`, value `(b << 16) | a`.\\n* `strong` – the first 16 bytes of SHA-256 over the block, base64 encoded.\\n\\nThe block size is chosen by the client: start at 4096 and double while\\n`size / blockSize > 32768`, capped at 1 MiB. This bounds the signature table to roughly\\n1.5 MiB of JSON.\\n\\n## Limits\\n\\n| Item | Limit |\\n| --- | --- |\\n| Frame payload | 16 MiB |\\n| Block size | 1 KiB .. 1 MiB |\\n| File size | configurable, default 8 GiB |\\n| Control string fields | validated (non-empty, bounded) |\\n| Socket idle timeout | 180 s |\\n\\n## Paths\\n\\nRemote paths are always relative to the server root, even if they begin with `/`.\\nLeading slashes are stripped, `..` segments and NUL bytes are rejected, and the\\nnormalised result must remain inside the root. `sync_begin` with `kind: \\"file\\"` targets\\na file path; with `kind: \\"dir\\"` it targets a directory that is created if missing.\\n","file_path":"rsyncj/docs/PROTOCOL.md"}'}}]}
